The Real Next Level: Building a Complete Trading Framework
We've now covered the individual indicators and their combined conceptual roles. The next useful step is not another indicator. It's learning how to make them cooperate without creating a Frankenstein strategy.
The source material supports the idea that each indicator measures a different aspect of the same market state. 
1. Start with a market-state classifier
Before thinking about entries, classify the market.
There are four major states:
[\boxed{\text{RANGE}}]
[\boxed{\text{TREND EXPANSION}}]
[\boxed{\text{TREND MATURITY}}]
[\boxed{\text{TRANSITION/EXHAUSTION}}]
Your indicators should help classify these states.

2. RANGE
Typical characteristics:
[ADX\downarrow]
[ATR\text{ low/stable}]
[RSI\approx30-70]
[CCI\rightarrow0]
[MACD\approx0]
Price repeatedly oscillates between boundaries.
The key insight:
[\boxed{\text{Don't use a trend-following interpretation in a range}}]
For example, RSI moving:
[35\rightarrow65]
can be much more meaningful in a range than in a powerful trend.
CCI can similarly oscillate:
[-150\rightarrow+150\rightarrow-150]
while price remains trapped.
ADX tells you:

This isn't behaving like a strong directional market.

That prevents a lot of unnecessary stupidity.

3. TREND BIRTH
Now imagine:
[ADX:12\rightarrow16\rightarrow21]
while:
[ATR\uparrow]
and price breaks structure.
Then:
[MACD\uparrow]
[CCI\uparrow]
[RSI>50]
[DeMarker\uparrow]
The important transition is:
[\boxed{Compression\rightarrowDirectional\ expansion}]
You aren't looking for seven green lights.
You're detecting a state transition.

4. TREND EXPANSION
This is your cleanest directional environment.
Suppose bullish:
Structure
[HH+HL+HH+HL]
RSI
[55\rightarrow62\rightarrow70]
MACD
[MACD\uparrow]
Histogram
[8\rightarrow15\rightarrow25]
CCI
[60\rightarrow130\rightarrow220]
ADX
[20\rightarrow27\rightarrow35]
ATR
[1.5\rightarrow2.0\rightarrow2.5]
DeMarker
[0.55\rightarrow0.65\rightarrow0.78]
The system is saying:
[\boxed{\text{Directional movement is expanding across multiple dimensions}}]
That is a much stronger observation than any individual crossover.

5. TREND MATURITY
Eventually the trend becomes obvious.
Suppose:
[ADX=48]
[RSI=78]
[CCI=250]
[ATR=4]
MACD strongly positive.
Everything looks powerful.
But this is precisely when you stop interpreting strength as an invitation to chase.
Why?
Because strong conditions can mean:
[\text{trend continuation}]
or:
[\text{late-stage trend}]
You need trajectory.

6. Detecting exhaustion
Now look for internal deterioration.
Price:
[HH\rightarrow HH\rightarrow HH]
But:
[RSI:80\rightarrow73\rightarrow67]
[MACD:220\rightarrow170\rightarrow120]
[Histogram:30\rightarrow18\rightarrow5]
[CCI:300\rightarrow240\rightarrow160]
[ADX:50\rightarrow43\rightarrow36]
[ATR:5\rightarrow4\rightarrow3]
[DeMarker:0.85\rightarrow0.76\rightarrow0.64]
Now you've got a trend that is still producing higher prices while its internal characteristics deteriorate.
That's the important signal.
Not:

SELL NOW.

Instead:
[\boxed{\text{Trend quality is deteriorating}}]
Then wait for price structure to actually fail.

7. The distinction between warning and trigger
This should become a permanent rule.
Indicators:
[\boxed{\text{WARNING}}]
Price structure:
[\boxed{\text{CONFIRMATION}}]
For example:
Indicators deteriorate:
[RSI\downarrow]
[MACD\downarrow]
[CCI\downarrow]
[DeMarker\downarrow]
Then price:
[HH\rightarrow LH]
and eventually:
[LL]
Now you have:
[\boxed{\text{structural transition}}]
This separation dramatically reduces premature reversal trades.

8. The most important setup: Pullback + reacceleration
This is one of the strongest ideas already present in the source's MACD material. 
Imagine a bullish trend.
Before pullback:
[MACD>0]
[ADX>25]
[RSI>60]
[CCI>100]
Then price pulls back.
You see:
[RSI:70\rightarrow50]
[CCI:180\rightarrow50]
[MACD\downarrow]
[Histogram:20\rightarrow5]
But:
[ADX\text{ remains elevated}]
and price maintains:
[HL]
Now momentum has reset without destroying structure.
Then:
[Histogram:5\rightarrow8\rightarrow15]
[MACD\uparrow]
[RSI:50\rightarrow58\rightarrow65]
[CCI:50\rightarrow100\rightarrow160]
That's:
[\boxed{Trend\rightarrowPullback\rightarrowMomentum\ reset\rightarrowReacceleration}]
This is vastly more informative than entering because RSI crossed 50.

9. Breakout framework
For a breakout, think:
[\boxed{\text{Compression}}]
then:
[\boxed{\text{Expansion}}]
Before breakout
[ATR\downarrow]
[ADX\text{ low}]
[MACD\approx0]
[CCI\approx0]
[RSI\approx50]
Breakout
[ATR\uparrow]
[ADX\uparrow]
[MACD\uparrow]
[Histogram\uparrow]
[CCI\uparrow]
[DeMarker\uparrow]
Price breaks:
[HH]
Now you have a coherent transition:
[\boxed{Compression\rightarrowBreakout\rightarrowDirectional\ expansion}]

10. False breakout framework
Now suppose price breaks upward but:
[ATR\uparrow]
while:
[ADX\text{ remains weak}]
and:
[MACD\text{ barely moves}]
[Histogram\text{ weak}]
[CCI\text{ spikes then collapses}]
[DeMarker\text{ fails to sustain highs}]
That gives:
[\boxed{Price\ expansion}]
without:
[\boxed{Directional\ organization}]
The breakout may fail.
Again, this isn't a magical prediction.
You're recognizing incomplete confirmation.

11. ATR is your risk layer
This deserves its own category.
Don't let your directional indicators decide how much capital you risk.
Use volatility for that.
Suppose:
[ATR=2]
and your stop model is:
[2ATR]
Then:
[Stop=4]
Now ATR doubles:
[ATR=4]
Same model:
[Stop=8]
If you want constant monetary risk:
[\boxed{Position\ Size\propto\frac{1}{ATR}}]
So volatility increases:
[\Rightarrow\text{position decreases}]
This is where ATR becomes a survival mechanism, which the source explicitly emphasizes. 

12. Don't normalize ATR incorrectly
Raw ATR isn't directly comparable between assets with wildly different prices.
Use:
[\boxed{ATR%=\frac{ATR}{Price}\times100}]
Example:
Asset A:
[Price=100,\ ATR=2]
[ATR%=2%]
Asset B:
[Price=1000,\ ATR=5]
[ATR%=0.5%]
Raw ATR:
[5>2]
Relative volatility:
[0.5%<2%]
This is a much better cross-asset comparison.

13. Indicator conflict is actually useful
Suppose:
[MACD\uparrow]
but:
[RSI\downarrow]
Don't immediately choose one.
Ask what each is measuring.
MACD:
[\text{momentum relationship}]
RSI:
[\text{recent gain/loss pressure}]
A conflict means the market may be transitioning.
Likewise:
[ATR\uparrow]
[ADX\downarrow]
means:
[\boxed{\text{more movement but less directional organization}}]
That is much more interesting than saying:

Indicators disagree.

They aren't disagreeing. They're describing different properties.

14. The hierarchy when indicators conflict
Use this order:
[\boxed{1.\ Price\ Structure}]
[\boxed{2.\ Market\ Regime}]
[\boxed{3.\ Volatility}]
[\boxed{4.\ Momentum}]
[\boxed{5.\ Oscillator\ details}]
Why?
Because an oscillator cannot magically override the actual structure of price.
If price is:
[HH+HL+HH]
and RSI reaches 75, you don't suddenly have a bear market.
If price breaks:
[HL\rightarrowLL]
then your bullish thesis actually has a structural problem.

15. Multi-timeframe framework
This is where the system becomes significantly stronger.
The source already gives a useful MACD example:
[Weekly=\text{bullish}]
[Daily=\text{bullish}]
[4H=\text{temporary bearish}]
That can represent a pullback rather than a full reversal. 
Generalize it.
Higher timeframe
Determine:
[\boxed{\text{Regime}}]
Middle timeframe
Determine:
[\boxed{\text{Trend structure}}]
Lower timeframe
Determine:
[\boxed{\text{Entry timing}}]
For example:
[1D:\text{bullish}]
[4H:\text{bullish pullback}]
[1H:\text{momentum reacceleration}]
This is much cleaner than trying to make a 5-minute RSI determine the entire market regime.

16. The complete state machine
Now we can formalize the whole framework.
                    ┌──────────────┐
                    │     RANGE    │
                    └──────┬───────┘
                           │
                    ATR expansion
                    ADX rising
                    structure break
                           │
                           ▼
                  ┌─────────────────┐
                  │ TREND EXPANSION │
                  └────────┬────────┘
                           │
                    ADX high
                    momentum strong
                           │
                           ▼
                  ┌─────────────────┐
                  │ TREND MATURITY  │
                  └────────┬────────┘
                           │
                 momentum deterioration
                 DeMarker deterioration
                 CCI contraction
                 ADX decline
                           │
                           ▼
                 ┌──────────────────┐
                 │    EXHAUSTION    │
                 └────────┬─────────┘
                          │
                    structure breaks
                          │
              ┌───────────┴───────────┐
              ▼                       ▼
           RANGE                   REVERSAL

That's the framework I'd eventually implement programmatically.

17. Turn indicators into features
Since you're aiming toward quantitative work, stop thinking exclusively in terms of:
[RSI>70]
Instead create variables.
For example:
RSI
[RSI_t]
[\Delta RSI_t=RSI_t-RSI_{t-1}]
[\Delta^2 RSI_t]
MACD
[MACD_t]
[Signal_t]
[Hist_t]
[\Delta Hist_t]
CCI
[CCI_t]
[\Delta CCI_t]
ADX
[ADX_t]
[\Delta ADX_t]
[DIspread_t=+DI_t--DI_t]
ATR
[ATR_t]
[ATR%_t=\frac{ATR_t}{Price_t}]
DeMarker
[DeM_t]
plus divergence/expansion-failure features.
Now you aren't building a collection of trading rules.
You're building a state representation.

18. The quantitative version
Eventually your model can represent the market at time (t) as:
[X_t=[S_t,RSI_t,\Delta RSI_t,MACD_t,Hist_t,CCI_t,ADX_t,DIspread_t,ATR_t,ATR%_t,DeM_t,\ldots]]
Then ask something scientifically testable:
[\boxed{P(R_{t+h}>R_t\mid X_t)}]
or perhaps:
[P(\text{trend continuation}\mid X_t)]
or:
[P(\text{volatility expansion}\mid X_t)]
Now we're no longer saying:

This chart looks bullish.

We're asking:

Under these measurable conditions, what historically happened next?

That's the transition from technical-analysis interpretation to quantitative hypothesis testing.

The master framework
When you look at any market, run:
[\boxed{\textbf{1. STRUCTURE}}]
What is price doing?
↓
[\boxed{\textbf{2. REGIME}}]
Trend or range?
↓
[\boxed{\textbf{3. ADX}}]
How organized is the movement?
↓
[\boxed{\textbf{4. ATR}}]
How intense is it?
↓
[\boxed{\textbf{5. MACD + HISTOGRAM}}]
What is momentum doing, and is it accelerating?
↓
[\boxed{\textbf{6. RSI}}]
How persistent is directional pressure?
↓
[\boxed{\textbf{7. CCI}}]
How displaced is price?
↓
[\boxed{\textbf{8. DEMARKER}}]
Can the move still expand?
↓
[\boxed{\textbf{9. PRICE CONFIRMATION}}]
Has structure actually confirmed the hypothesis?
↓
[\boxed{\textbf{10. ATR-BASED RISK}}]
Can the position survive being wrong?
That last one matters more than traders like to admit. The source explicitly calls ATR a survival tool rather than merely another signal generator. 
The next logical lesson is no longer another indicator. It is a full set of concrete trading setups and failure cases, where we take this framework candle-by-candle and determine exactly what each indicator is allowed to contribute.

Lesson 21: Concrete Trading Setups + Failure Cases
We now stop treating the indicators as a collection of interesting mathematical gadgets and start treating them as a decision system. Humanity has invented seven ways to measure the same candle, so naturally the next step is figuring out when each one is actually allowed to speak.
The core rule:

Price structure determines what is happening. Indicators determine how healthy, strong, weak, or unstable that move is.


1. Setup A: Trend Continuation
Market structure
Suppose price is producing:
        HH
       /  \
      /    \       HH
     /      \     /
    /        HL /
   /
  HL

We have:

Higher High
Higher Low
another attempt at a Higher High

That is already bullish structure.
Now the indicators tell us whether the trend is healthy.
Bullish continuation profile



Indicator
What we want



Price
HH → HL


RSI
Generally >50 and rising


MACD
Above signal / rising


Histogram
Expanding during impulse


CCI
Positive and recovering


ADX
Elevated or rising


ATR
Stable or expanding


DeMarker
>0.5 and supporting expansion


The important thing is not that every box is green.
It's the sequence.
Example
Price:
100 → 105 → 102 → 110

During the pullback:
RSI:       68 → 54
MACD:      rising → contracts
Histogram: +2.0 → +0.4
CCI:       +150 → +30
ADX:       31 → 29
ATR:       2.4 → 2.2

This isn't necessarily bearish.
It's exactly what you might expect from a healthy pullback.
Then price begins accelerating:
RSI:       54 → 61
MACD:      turns upward
Histogram: +0.4 → +1.1
CCI:       +30 → +110
ADX:       29 → 32
DeMarker:  rises

That is much more interesting.
The actual signal
Not:

RSI crossed 50. BUY.

Instead:

Bullish structure survived → momentum contracted → selling pressure failed to break structure → momentum reaccelerated.

That is a completely different level of reasoning.

2. The Pullback Trap
Here's where people donate money to the market.
Imagine:
        HH
       /  \
      /    \
     /      \
    /        \
   /          \
  HL           ↓

You see RSI fall from 70 → 45.
You conclude:

"Trend reversal!"

No.
RSI falling during a pullback is normal.
The important question is:
Did price structure fail?
Compare two situations.
Situation A
HH
 \
  HL
   \
    higher low holds

Trend structure survives.
Potential continuation.
Situation B
HH
 \
  HL
   \
    LL
     \
      LH

Now structure has actually changed.
That's materially different.

3. Setup B: Trend Birth
This is one of the most useful setups.
Before the move:
Price:       compressed
ADX:         low
ATR:         low
MACD:        near zero
Histogram:   small
RSI:         ~50
CCI:         near zero
DeMarker:    ~0.5

Essentially:

Nobody has established directional control.

Then:
Price breaks structure
       ↓
ATR expands
       ↓
MACD moves
       ↓
Histogram expands
       ↓
CCI displaces
       ↓
ADX begins rising

This is the transition:
RANGE
  ↓
COMPRESSION
  ↓
TREND BIRTH
  ↓
TREND EXPANSION

The critical detail is ADX lag.
ADX is derived from directional movement and smoothing, so it doesn't have to explode at the exact moment price breaks out.
Therefore:

Don't demand ADX confirmation before the move begins.

Instead, look for ADX to confirm that the move is becoming organized.

4. Setup C: Breakout
A strong breakout generally has a sequence like:
Before breakout
ATR       ↓
ADX       low
MACD      near 0
Histogram small
RSI       ~50
CCI       ~0

Then:
Breakout candle
Price     breaks resistance
ATR       ↑
MACD      ↑
Histogram ↑
CCI       ↑
RSI       ↑
DeMarker  ↑

Then:
Follow-through
Price     HH
ADX       ↑
ATR       remains elevated
MACD      remains positive
Histogram stays positive

That is much stronger than simply:

"Price crossed resistance."


5. False Breakout
Now the fun part.
Markets love pretending.
Suppose resistance is:
──────────── resistance
       ↑
       │ breakout
       │

Price briefly breaks above it.
But:
ATR        ↑
ADX        barely moves
MACD       weak
Histogram  spikes → collapses
CCI        +180 → +40
DeMarker   fails to sustain
RSI        fails to maintain strength

Price then falls back below resistance.
This is a failed expansion.
The crucial concept:

A breakout is not proven by crossing a level. It is proven by the market's ability to sustain expansion after crossing it.

That is why DeMarker becomes particularly interesting here.
Price made a new high.
But expansion capability failed.

6. Setup D: Trend Exhaustion
This is where the indicators become useful as diagnostic instruments.
Imagine:
Price:

        HH
       /
      HH
     /
    HH
   /
  HH

Everything still looks bullish.
But underneath:
RSI        72 → 65 → 59
MACD       declining
Histogram  shrinking
CCI        +180 → +90 → +30
ADX        42 → 37
DeMarker   declining
ATR        declining

This tells us:

Price is still moving upward, but the internal characteristics of the move are deteriorating.

That is exhaustion risk.
It is NOT:

"SHORT NOW."

That's a critical distinction.

7. Exhaustion vs Reversal
These are different states.
Exhaustion
Trend weakening
      ↓
Momentum weakening
      ↓
Expansion weakening

But price structure may still be intact.
Reversal
Trend weakening
      ↓
Structure breaks
      ↓
LH
      ↓
LL

Therefore:

Indicators can warn about a reversal. Price structure confirms the reversal.

This single distinction prevents an enormous amount of premature countertrend trading.

8. Setup E: Range Trading
Now suppose:
Price:

Resistance ─────────────
       ↑      ↓
       │      │
       ↓      ↑
Support ────────────────

Indicators:
ADX        < ~20
ATR        low/stable
RSI        oscillating
MACD       near zero
CCI        repeatedly crossing zero
DeMarker   oscillating around 0.5

This is fundamentally different from a trend.
You shouldn't interpret:
RSI 70

the same way in a range and a strong bull trend.
In a range
RSI 70 may indicate:

price is approaching the upper side of the range.

In a strong trend
RSI 70 may indicate:

strong persistent buying.

Same indicator.
Different state.
This is why regime classification comes before indicator interpretation.

9. The Most Important Failure Case: Indicator Voting
Imagine:
RSI        bullish
MACD       bullish
CCI        bullish
DeMarker   bullish
ADX        bearish
ATR        neutral

Do NOT calculate:
4 bullish
1 bearish

BUY

That's kindergarten statistics wearing a tie.
Each indicator measures a different dimension.
Instead ask:
Price structure → ?
Trend organization → ?
Volatility → ?
Momentum → ?
Acceleration → ?
Displacement → ?
Expansion → ?

For example:
Structure:       bullish
ADX:             weak
ATR:             low
Momentum:        bullish
Expansion:       bullish

Interpretation:

Bullish movement exists, but directional organization hasn't fully developed.

That might represent trend birth, not an established trend.

10. Setup F: Strong Trend + Falling ATR
This one catches people constantly.
Suppose:
Price     HH → HH → HH
ADX       35 → 38 → 40
RSI       61 → 63 → 65
MACD      rising
Histogram positive
ATR       3.0 → 2.7 → 2.5

Someone sees:

ATR falling = bearish.

Wrong.
ATR has no direction.
It measures movement magnitude.
The market can become:

more directionally organized while becoming less volatile.

That's actually a potentially very healthy trend.
Think:
Early trend:
large chaotic candles

Later trend:
smaller but consistently directional candles

ATR falls.
ADX rises.
Price continues.
That's controlled trend behavior.

11. Setup G: High ATR + Low ADX
Another extremely important state:
ATR       ↑↑↑
ADX       low/falling
Price     violent movement
MACD      alternating
RSI       whipsawing

This means:

High movement does not necessarily mean strong trend.

Possible environment:
news shock
panic
liquidation
gap
violent range
regime transition

This is:
VOLATILITY SHOCK
Not necessarily:
TREND
That distinction matters enormously for position sizing.
If ATR doubles, your stop distance may need to increase.
If your risk per trade stays constant, your position size should generally decrease.
Conceptually:
[PositionSize \propto \frac{RiskBudget}{StopDistance}]
and if:
[StopDistance \propto ATR]
then approximately:
[PositionSize \propto \frac{1}{ATR}]
So volatility isn't just an indicator.
It directly affects risk engineering.

12. Setup H: DeMarker Expansion Failure
This is one of the more advanced combinations.
Price:
Previous HH
     \
      \
       New HH

But:
DeMarker:

       /\ 
      /  \
     /    \__
          lower high

Price made a higher high.
DeMarker did not.
Now add:
MACD        declining
Histogram   shrinking
RSI         declining
CCI         declining
ADX         declining

Now the evidence becomes much more interesting.
You have:

Price expansion without equivalent internal expansion.

That is an exhaustion warning.
Still, you wait for structure.
If price subsequently forms:
HH
 \
  LH
   \
    LL

the warning has become structural confirmation.

13. The Full Setup Hierarchy
Every trade idea should now pass through this hierarchy:
                 PRICE STRUCTURE
                       │
                       ▼
                 MARKET REGIME
                       │
              ┌────────┴────────┐
              ▼                 ▼
            TREND              RANGE
              │                 │
              ▼                 ▼
           ADX/ATR          ADX/ATR
              │                 │
              ▼                 ▼
         MOMENTUM              OSCILLATION
       MACD + RSI            RSI + CCI
              │
              ▼
          ACCELERATION
          Histogram
              │
              ▼
          DISPLACEMENT
             CCI
              │
              ▼
        EXPANSION CAPACITY
           DeMarker
              │
              ▼
             RISK
             ATR

That is the architecture.

14. The Three Questions Every Indicator Must Answer
Instead of asking:

"Is RSI bullish?"

Ask:
Question 1
What does this indicator measure?
Question 2
What changed relative to its recent state?
Question 3
Does that change agree with price structure and the current regime?
For example:
RSI
Bad interpretation:

RSI = 72, therefore sell.

Better:

RSI has fallen from 78 → 72 while price makes a new high.

Then:

Is this merely cooling, or is directional pressure genuinely deteriorating?

Now compare MACD, histogram, CCI, DeMarker, ADX and structure.
That's actual analysis.

15. Your First Professional-Level Rule
From this point forward, stop recording indicator values as isolated facts.
Instead record transitions.
Not:
RSI = 63
MACD = 1.4
CCI = 112
ADX = 31
ATR = 2.3

But:
RSI:       55 → 63 ↑
MACD:      0.8 → 1.4 ↑
Histogram: 0.2 → 0.7 ↑
CCI:       40 → 112 ↑
ADX:       26 → 31 ↑
ATR:       2.0 → 2.3 ↑
DeMarker:  0.56 → 0.68 ↑

The derivatives of the indicators often contain more useful information than their absolute values.
Conceptually:
[\Delta RSI_t = RSI_t-RSI_{t-1}]
[\Delta MACD_t = MACD_t-MACD_{t-1}]
[\Delta H_t = H_t-H_{t-1}]
and even:
[\Delta^2 H_t
\Delta H_t-\Delta H_{t-1}]
Now we're moving toward quantitative modeling.

16. The Market-State Model
All of this eventually collapses into a state machine:
                 ┌──────────────┐
                 │    RANGE     │
                 └──────┬───────┘
                        │
                   compression
                        │
                        ▼
                 ┌──────────────┐
                 │ TREND BIRTH  │
                 └──────┬───────┘
                        │
                  confirmation
                        │
                        ▼
              ┌───────────────────┐
              │ TREND EXPANSION   │
              └─────────┬─────────┘
                        │
                   deterioration
                        │
                        ▼
              ┌───────────────────┐
              │ TREND MATURITY    │
              └─────────┬─────────┘
                        │
                    exhaustion
                        │
                        ▼
              ┌───────────────────┐
              │    TRANSITION     │
              └──────┬───────┬────┘
                     │       │
                 reversal   range
                     │       │
                     ▼       ▼
                  TREND     RANGE

This is the bridge between:
technical analysis
and
quantitative modeling.
Eventually we stop saying:

"This chart looks bullish."

and start asking:
[P(R_{t+h}>0\mid X_t)]
where (X_t) is the vector of market-state features.
That's where the subject gets considerably more interesting.

Next lesson
Lesson 22: Quantifying the Setups
We'll turn these setups into actual measurable conditions:

Define trend mathematically
Define compression mathematically
Define expansion mathematically
Build indicator feature vectors
Create bullish/bearish state scores without stupid vote-counting
Define entry, invalidation and exit conditions
Measure false breakouts
Calculate expectancy
Introduce R-multiples
Build a first rules-based strategy
Then attack it with failure cases and overfitting

That is where the indicators stop being chart decorations and become inputs to a real quantitative system.

Lesson 22: Quantifying the Setups
Now we turn the framework into something a quant could actually test. No more "the chart feels strong," because feelings are notoriously poor statistical estimators.
The goal is to convert:

Price + RSI + MACD + Histogram + CCI + ADX + ATR + DeMarker

into a measurable market-state representation.

1. First: Define What We Are Predicting
Suppose current candle is (t).
We want to know whether price will move upward over some future horizon (h).
Define the forward return:
[R_{t,h}=\frac{P_{t+h}-P_t}{P_t}]
where:

(P_t) = current price
(P_{t+h}) = price (h) candles later
(R_{t,h}) = future return

Then the quantitative question becomes:
[P(R_{t,h}>0\mid X_t)]
where (X_t) contains everything we know at time (t).
For example:
[X_t=[RSI_t,MACD_t,H_t,CCI_t,ADX_t,ATR_t,DeM_t]]
But raw values aren't enough.

2. Absolute Values vs Changes
Consider:
[RSI=60]
By itself, that's not particularly informative.
But:
[RSI_{t-5}=48]
[RSI_t=60]
gives:
[\Delta RSI = 12]
Now we know directional pressure has increased.
So our feature vector should contain both level and change.
For example:
[X_t=[RSI,\Delta RSI,MACD,\Delta MACD,H,\Delta H,CCI,\Delta CCI,ADX,\Delta ADX,ATR,\Delta ATR,DeM,\Delta DeM]]

3. Normalize What Needs Normalizing
Here's a subtle problem.
Suppose:
Stock A:
ATR = 2

Stock B:
ATR = 200

Those numbers mean almost nothing without knowing price scale.
Instead use:
[ATR%=\frac{ATR}{P}\times100]
If:
[P=100]
and:
[ATR=2]
then:
[ATR%=2%]
This makes volatility much more comparable across instruments.

4. Measuring Trend Mathematically
We need a definition of trend that doesn't rely on eyeballing candles.
One simple structural approach:
Bullish structure
[HH_t > HH_{t-1}]
and
[HL_t > HL_{t-1}]
where:

(HH) = higher-high structure
(HL) = higher-low structure

Similarly bearish:
[LH_t < LH_{t-1}]
[LL_t < LL_{t-1}]
But there's an even simpler quantitative approximation.
Define a moving-window price slope:
[\beta_t]
from a linear regression:
[P_i=\alpha+\beta i+\epsilon_i]
If:
[\beta>0]
price has positive local trend.
If:
[\beta<0]
price has negative local trend.
But don't confuse:

positive slope

with:

strong trend.

That's where ADX becomes useful.

5. Trend Strength as a Combination
Consider:
[TrendScore
f(\beta,ADX)]
Conceptually:
Price slope ↑
+
ADX ↑
=
stronger directional organization

But:
Price slope ↑
+
ADX ↓
=
movement upward with deteriorating organization

That distinction is much more valuable than simply asking whether MACD is positive.

6. Quantifying Momentum
For MACD:
[MACD_t=EMA_{12,t}-EMA_{26,t}]
Define momentum change:
[\Delta MACD_t=MACD_t-MACD_{t-1}]
Then histogram:
[H_t=MACD_t-Signal_t]
and:
[\Delta H_t=H_t-H_{t-1}]
Now you can distinguish:
Positive momentum
[MACD>0]
Increasing momentum
[\Delta MACD>0]
Increasing acceleration of histogram
[\Delta H>0]
These aren't identical concepts.

7. Example
Suppose:
[MACD=1.2]
and:
[MACD_{t-1}=1.0]
Then:
[\Delta MACD=0.2]
Momentum is increasing.
But suppose:
[H_t=0.4]
and:
[H_{t-1}=0.6]
Then:
[\Delta H=-0.2]
So you have:

MACD still rising, but histogram weakening.

That's a very different state from:
MACD ↑
Histogram ↑

The first can represent momentum persistence with declining acceleration.
The second represents increasing momentum and acceleration.

8. Quantifying CCI Displacement
CCI gives us displacement relative to its recent typical-price equilibrium.
Rather than simply:

CCI > +100

track:
[CCI_t]
and:
[\Delta CCI_t]
Then distinguish:
CCI = +150
ΔCCI = +30

from:
CCI = +150
ΔCCI = -40

Both are above +100.
But their dynamics are opposite.
The first:

displacement increasing.

The second:

displacement contracting.


9. ADX Becomes a Regime Feature
Use:
[ADX_t]
and:
[\Delta ADX_t]
Example:
State A
[ADX=14,\quad\Delta ADX=+4]
Trend organization is low but increasing.
Potential trend birth.
State B
[ADX=40,\quad\Delta ADX=+5]
Strong organization and strengthening.
Potential trend expansion.
State C
[ADX=40,\quad\Delta ADX=-5]
Trend strength is deteriorating.
Potential maturity/exhaustion.
Same ADX value.
Completely different trajectory.

10. ATR Gives the Volatility Regime
Define:
[V_t=\frac{ATR_t}{P_t}]
Then track:
[\Delta V_t=V_t-V_{t-1}]
Now you can classify:



ATR state
Interpretation



Low + falling
Compression


Low + rising
Expansion beginning


High + rising
Volatility shock / expansion


High + falling
Volatility contraction


Again:
ATR does not tell you direction.
It tells you the scale of movement.

11. Building a State Vector
Now combine everything.
For a bullish candidate:
[X_t=[Trend,RSI,\Delta RSI,MACD,\Delta MACD,H,\Delta H,CCI,\Delta CCI,ADX,\Delta ADX,ATR%,\Delta ATR%,DeM,\Delta DeM]]
You can think of this as a market state vector.
The market isn't simply:

bullish/bearish.

It's more like:
Direction
Momentum
Acceleration
Displacement
Organization
Volatility
Expansion
Structure

That's much closer to how a quantitative system should represent it.

12. Constructing a Bullish State Score
We can create a preliminary research score.
For example:
[S_t=w_1T_t+w_2M_t+w_3A_t+w_4D_t+w_5O_t+w_6E_t]
where:

(T) = trend structure
(M) = momentum
(A) = acceleration
(D) = displacement
(O) = organization
(E) = expansion

and:
[w_i]
are weights.
Important:
Do not assume equal weights are optimal.
They're merely a starting hypothesis.

13. Example State
Suppose normalized features give:
Trend         +1
Momentum      +0.8
Acceleration  +0.6
Displacement  +0.7
Organization  +0.5
Expansion     +0.8

Then with equal weights:
[S=\frac{1+0.8+0.6+0.7+0.5+0.8}{6}]
[S=0.733]
You could classify that as a strong bullish state.
But here's the important part:
This score is NOT automatically a trading signal.
It is a feature engineering experiment.
We then test whether high (S_t) actually corresponds to higher future returns.

14. The Real Quantitative Test
Take historical candles.
For every candle (t):

Calculate the indicators.
Calculate the feature vector (X_t).
Calculate future return (R_{t,h}).
Store both.

You eventually obtain:



Time
RSI
ΔRSI
MACD
ADX
ATR%
DeM
Future Return



(t_1)
61
+4
1.2
28
1.8%
.64
+1.2%


(t_2)
54
-3
.8
31
1.7%
.55
-0.4%


(t_3)
67
+7
1.8
35
2.1%
.71
+2.0%


Now we're doing empirical research rather than chart astrology.

15. R-Multiples
Now introduce one of the most important concepts in systematic trading.
Suppose you risk:
[$100]
on a trade.
Your stop is hit.
You lose:
[-$100=-1R]
If you make:
[$200]
that's:
[+2R]
If you make:
[$300]
that's:
[+3R]
So:
[R=\text{initial risk}]
and trade return becomes normalized by risk.

16. Expectancy
Suppose your strategy has:

Win probability = (p)
Average win = (W)
Loss probability = (1-p)
Average loss = (L)

Then:
[E=pW-(1-p)L]
Example:
[p=0.45]
[W=2R]
[L=1R]
Then:
[E=(0.45)(2)-(0.55)(1)]
[E=0.90-0.55]
[\boxed{E=+0.35R}]
So the strategy can be profitable despite winning less than half its trades.
This is why win rate alone is almost useless.

17. Failure Cases Become Quantifiable
Remember our false breakout?
Instead of saying:

"It looked like a fake breakout."

Define it.
For example:
Breakout occurs
↓
Price exceeds resistance
↓
Within N candles price closes back below resistance
↓
Follow-through return < threshold

Now you can count:
[N_{false}]
and:
[N_{total}]
Then:
[P(FalseBreakout)
\frac{N_{false}}{N_{total}}]
Now the hypothesis can actually be tested.

18. The Dangerous Part: Overfitting
Suppose you discover:

"RSI > 61.37 + ADX > 27.82 + CCI > 104.6 gives amazing results."

Congratulations.
You may have discovered absolutely nothing.
You might simply have optimized parameters against historical noise.
This is overfitting.
The model has learned the sample rather than the underlying relationship.
A robust strategy should survive:

different time periods
different assets
different market regimes
slightly different parameters
unseen data


19. Train vs Test
Your historical data should be separated.
Conceptually:
Historical data
       │
       ├──────────────┐
       ▼              ▼
   Training        Testing
     data            data
       │              │
       ▼              │
Develop model         │
       │              │
       └──────────────┘
              │
              ▼
        Evaluate once

The test set is supposed to represent data the model didn't get to learn from.
Otherwise you're basically grading your own exam after secretly writing the answer key.

20. The Bigger Picture
We've now transformed the original indicator framework:
RSI
MACD
CCI
ADX
ATR
DeMarker

into:
             MARKET DATA
                  │
                  ▼
          INDICATOR FEATURES
                  │
                  ▼
         FEATURE TRANSFORMS
       ┌──────────┼──────────┐
       ▼          ▼          ▼
      Level      Change    Volatility
       │          │          │
       └──────────┼──────────┘
                  ▼
             STATE VECTOR
                  │
                  ▼
           REGIME CLASSIFIER
                  │
                  ▼
             TRADE MODEL
                  │
                  ▼
            RISK ENGINE
                  │
                  ▼
             EXECUTION
                  │
                  ▼
             PERFORMANCE
                  │
                  ▼
              RESEARCH
                  │
                  └──────► iterate

This is the beginning of an actual systematic trading architecture.

The key lesson
Don't ask:

"What does RSI say?"

Ask:

"What measurable information does RSI contribute to the current market state that isn't already captured by the other features?"

That question is much closer to quantitative research.
And eventually we can test whether RSI actually contributes incremental predictive information after MACD, CCI, ADX, ATR and price structure are already known.
That leads directly into the next level:
Lesson 23: Building the First Quantitative Strategy
We'll construct an actual ruleset from the framework:
[\text{Regime}\rightarrow\text{Setup}\rightarrow\text{Entry}\rightarrow\text{Stop}\rightarrow\text{Position Size}\rightarrow\text{Exit}\rightarrow\text{Expectancy}]
Then we'll deliberately try to break the strategy, because a strategy that only works when nobody punches it is not a strategy.

Lesson 23: Build the First Quantitative Strategy
Now we stop merely describing market states and actually construct a testable strategy.
The strategy we'll build is deliberately simple. The objective isn't to discover the Holy Grail, because apparently markets have declined to provide one. The objective is to learn how a quantitative strategy is assembled so every assumption can later be tested.

1. Strategy Architecture
A proper strategy has separate layers:
MARKET DATA
    ↓
FEATURES
    ↓
REGIME
    ↓
SETUP
    ↓
ENTRY
    ↓
RISK
    ↓
EXIT
    ↓
PERFORMANCE

Do not mix these together.
For example:

"RSI > 50, therefore buy."

is not a complete strategy.
We need to know:

What market condition?
What setup?
Where exactly do we enter?
Where are we wrong?
How much do we risk?
When do we exit?
What happens statistically afterward?


2. Strategy: Bullish Trend Continuation
We'll build our first strategy around the setup from Lesson 21.
Required market structure
We want:
[HH_t > HH_{t-1}]
and:
[HL_t > HL_{t-1}]
So price must be structurally bullish.
This is our primary filter.

3. Regime Filter
We don't want to trade every bullish-looking move.
Require:
[ADX > 25]
and:
[\Delta ADX > 0]
Conceptually:

The market has directional organization, and that organization is increasing.

The exact 25 threshold isn't sacred. It's simply our initial research parameter.
Later we test whether 20, 25, 30, etc. actually matter.

4. Momentum Condition
Require:
[MACD > Signal]
and:
[\Delta MACD > 0]
This means:

MACD is on the bullish side.
Momentum is still increasing.

But we're not entering yet.
Because blindly entering whenever MACD is bullish is exactly how indicators become expensive wallpaper.

5. Acceleration Condition
Use the histogram:
[H=MACD-Signal]
Require:
[\Delta H>0]
This means histogram momentum is increasing.
So now:
Structure     bullish
ADX           strengthening
MACD          strengthening
Histogram     expanding

We have directional organization plus momentum.

6. RSI Condition
Require:
[50<RSI<70]
and:
[\Delta RSI>0]
Why not simply:
[RSI>70?]
Because 70 isn't a magical ceiling.
In a strong trend, RSI can remain above 70 for extended periods.
For this particular continuation strategy, we're looking for:

bullish pressure that is recovering/strengthening without requiring an extreme RSI reading.


7. CCI Condition
Require:
[CCI>0]
and:
[\Delta CCI>0]
This gives us:

price displacement is positive and increasing.

Notice how each indicator has a specific responsibility:
Price structure → direction
ADX             → organization
MACD            → momentum
Histogram       → acceleration
RSI             → directional pressure
CCI             → displacement


8. DeMarker
Require:
[DeM>0.5]
and:
[\Delta DeM>0]
This indicates that upward expansion capability is currently supportive.
Again, it is not:

DeMarker > 0.5 = buy.

It's one component of a specific state.

9. Complete Entry Condition
Our initial research rule becomes:
LONG candidate if:
[Structure_{bullish}]
AND
[ADX>25]
AND
[\Delta ADX>0]
AND
[MACD>Signal]
AND
[\Delta MACD>0]
AND
[\Delta H>0]
AND
[50<RSI<70]
AND
[\Delta RSI>0]
AND
[CCI>0]
AND
[\Delta CCI>0]
AND
[DeM>0.5]
AND
[\Delta DeM>0]
That's intentionally restrictive.
We're not claiming this is optimal.
We're creating a baseline hypothesis.

10. But There's a Problem
Look at what we just did.
We created a strategy with approximately eleven conditions.
That can easily become:

"I added enough filters until historical charts looked beautiful."

That's dangerous.
Why?
Because every additional condition creates another opportunity to accidentally fit historical noise.
Therefore, the next step is not:

Add five more indicators.

It's:

Test whether each feature actually contributes information.


11. Entry Timing
Suppose all conditions become true at candle (t).
We have two possibilities.
Enter at close
[Entry=P_t]
Simple.
But unrealistic if you're assuming you somehow know the closing price before the candle closes.
Enter next candle
[Entry=P_{t+1}]
This is generally cleaner for a basic backtest.
You calculate the signal using candle (t), then execute using candle (t+1).
This prevents a common form of look-ahead bias.

12. Look-Ahead Bias
Suppose you calculate:
RSI at candle t
MACD at candle t

and then pretend you bought at the opening price of candle (t).
Impossible.
You didn't know the completed candle yet.
That uses future information.
The correct sequence is:
Candle t closes
      ↓
Calculate indicators
      ↓
Generate signal
      ↓
Execute at t+1

This tiny detail can completely invalidate a backtest.

13. Stop Loss
Now ATR enters the risk engine.
Suppose:
[ATR=2]
Set:
[StopDistance=1.5\times ATR]
Therefore:
[StopDistance=3]
If entry is:
[P=100]
then:
[Stop=97]
Our initial risk is:
[3]
per unit.

14. Position Sizing
Suppose your strategy's risk budget is:
[$100]
and each unit risks:
[$3]
Then:
[PositionSize=\frac{100}{3}]
[\boxed{33.33}]
Depending on the asset, you would round appropriately.
This is a crucial concept:

Position size changes with volatility.

If ATR doubles:
[ATR=4]
and stop distance remains:
[1.5ATR=6]
then:
[PositionSize=\frac{100}{6}]
[\approx16.67]
Same risk budget.
Smaller position.
That's proper volatility-adjusted sizing.

15. Take Profit
For our first experiment, use a simple:
[2R]
target.
If:
[R=3]
then:
[TargetDistance=6]
Entry:
[100]
Stop:
[97]
Target:
[106]
So:
Entry      100
Stop        97     = -1R
Target     106     = +2R

This gives us a clean payoff structure.

16. Exit Conditions
We can use three types of exits.
A. Stop
Price reaches:
[Entry-1.5ATR]
Trade:
[-1R]
B. Target
Price reaches:
[Entry+3ATR]
Trade:
[+2R]
C. Signal failure
For example, if the structural thesis disappears:
Bullish structure
      ↓
Lower high
      ↓
Lower low

then the original continuation thesis has failed.
This is different from merely having RSI fall.

17. Why Structure Should Control Invalidation
Imagine:
Price:

       HH
      /
     HL
    /
   ↑ entry

Then RSI falls:
[65\rightarrow52]
MACD weakens.
Histogram contracts.
Would you immediately exit?
Not necessarily.
The market may simply be pulling back.
But if:
HL breaks
↓
LH forms
↓
LL forms

then your original bullish structural hypothesis is invalidated.
That is much stronger evidence.

18. Now We Can Backtest
For every historical candle:
1. Calculate features
2. Determine structure
3. Determine regime
4. Check entry conditions
5. If signal:
       enter next candle
6. Calculate ATR stop
7. Calculate position size
8. Track trade
9. Exit at stop / target / invalidation
10. Record R-multiple

Your dataset eventually becomes:



Trade
Entry
Stop
Target
Result



1
100
97
106
+2R


2
110
107
116
-1R


3
120
117
126
+2R


4
130
127
136
-1R


Then calculate performance.

19. Expectancy
Suppose 100 trades produce:

45 winners
55 losers
winners = +2R
losers = -1R

Then:
[E=(0.45)(2)-(0.55)(1)]
[E=0.35R]
Expected return per trade:
[\boxed{+0.35R}]
If:
[R=$100]
then theoretical average:
[$35/trade]
before transaction costs, slippage, taxes, financing, execution effects, and all the other delightful ways reality charges rent.

20. Profit Factor
Another useful metric:
[ProfitFactor=\frac{GrossProfit}{GrossLoss}]
Suppose:
[GrossProfit=180R]
and:
[GrossLoss=100R]
Then:
[PF=1.8]
A value above 1 means gross profits exceed gross losses.
But again, one metric is never enough.

21. Maximum Drawdown
Suppose your equity curve is:
100
110
120
115
108
112
125

The peak reached:
[120]
Then fell to:
[108]
Drawdown:
[120-108=12]
Percentage drawdown:
[\frac{120-108}{120}\times100]
[=10%]
Maximum drawdown tells you something win rate cannot:

How painful the path to profitability can be.

A strategy earning +40% with a 35% drawdown is very different from one earning +40% with an 8% drawdown.

22. Now Attack the Strategy
This is the part most retail backtests conveniently forget.
We deliberately look for situations where the strategy should fail.
Failure 1: Sideways market
ADX low
ATR low
price oscillating

Our trend strategy should ideally produce few trades.
Failure 2: Volatility shock
ATR ↑↑
ADX weak
price whipsaws

The trend conditions may trigger and immediately fail.
Failure 3: Late trend
ADX high
price extended
RSI extreme
MACD weakening
DeMarker weakening

Continuation entries may have poor reward/risk.
Failure 4: News gap
Indicators calculated from historical candles may react too slowly.
Failure 5: Regime transition
A strategy optimized for stable trends can behave terribly when the market changes from:
trend → range

or:
range → violent expansion


23. The Most Important Experiment
Don't just test:

"Does the strategy make money?"

Test:
What happens when each feature is removed?
Run:
Model A:
All features

Then:
Model B:
Remove RSI

Then:
Model C:
Remove CCI

Then:
Model D:
Remove DeMarker

Then:
Model E:
Remove ADX

Compare:

expectancy
profit factor
drawdown
trade count
Sharpe-like risk-adjusted measures
stability across regimes

This is ablation testing.
If removing DeMarker changes nothing, then perhaps DeMarker isn't adding much incremental information to this particular strategy.
That's a far more meaningful conclusion than:

"DeMarker is a good indicator."


24. Your First Quantitative Principle
An indicator does not deserve a place in a strategy because it sounds intelligent.
It deserves a place because:
[\boxed{\text{It contributes measurable incremental information}}]
after accounting for information already present in the other features.
That is the mindset shift.

25. Strategy v0.1
Our first research specification is therefore:
Market
Any liquid instrument/timeframe for initial testing.
Direction
Long only.
Regime
[ADX>25,\quad \Delta ADX>0]
Structure
Bullish HH/HL structure.
Momentum
[MACD>Signal,\quad \Delta MACD>0]
Acceleration
[\Delta Histogram>0]
Pressure
[50<RSI<70,\quad \Delta RSI>0]
Displacement
[CCI>0,\quad \Delta CCI>0]
Expansion
[DeM>0.5,\quad\Delta DeM>0]
Risk
[Stop=Entry-1.5ATR]
Target
[Target=Entry+3ATR]
Therefore:
[Reward:Risk=2:1]
Execution
Signal on candle (t), execution no earlier than (t+1).
This is now a fully specified hypothesis.
Not a proven strategy.
That's an important distinction.

26. The Next Step Gets More Interesting
We have a problem with our current strategy.
It treats every feature as a hard Boolean:
RSI > 50       TRUE/FALSE
CCI > 0        TRUE/FALSE
ADX > 25       TRUE/FALSE

That throws away information.
For example:
ADX = 25.01

and:
ADX = 55

are both simply:
TRUE

That's crude.
The next evolution is to stop thinking in binary conditions and construct continuous market-state features and scores.
We'll move from:
[TRUE/FALSE]
to:
[X_t\in\mathbb{R}]
and eventually toward:
[P(R_{t+h}>0\mid X_t)]
That is Lesson 24: Continuous State Modeling, Feature Normalization, and Signal Scoring.

next


ChatGPT:
Lesson 24: Continuous State Modeling
Our previous strategy used crude switches:
[RSI>50 \Rightarrow 1]
[RSI\le50 \Rightarrow 0]
That works for a first prototype, but it throws away information. A market at RSI 50.1 and one at RSI 68 are treated identically. Humanity has managed to build computers capable of floating-point arithmetic and then immediately decided to use TRUE/FALSE. Let's improve it.

1. From Rules to Features
Instead of:
[RSI>50]
we create a continuous feature.
For example:
[RSI_f=\frac{RSI-50}{20}]
So approximately:



RSI
Feature



30
-1.0


40
-0.5


50
0


60
+0.5


70
+1.0


Now RSI carries magnitude and direction.
The exact transformation isn't sacred. It is a modeling choice.

2. Why Normalization Matters
Consider these raw features:
[RSI=63]
[CCI=127]
[ADX=31]
[ATR%=2.4]
Their numerical scales are completely different.
If we combine them directly:
[Score=RSI+CCI+ADX+ATR]
CCI dominates simply because its numerical scale is larger.
That's not intelligence.
That's arithmetic bullying.
We therefore transform features onto comparable scales.

3. Z-Score Normalization
A common transformation is:
[Z_t=\frac{X_t-\mu}{\sigma}]
where:

(X_t) = current feature
(\mu) = historical mean
(\sigma) = historical standard deviation
(Z_t) = standardized value

Example:
Suppose ADX historically has:
[\mu=25]
[\sigma=8]
and currently:
[ADX=41]
Then:
[Z_{ADX}
\frac{41-25}{8}
2]
So current ADX is approximately 2 standard deviations above its historical mean.
That is much more informative than simply saying:

ADX = 41.


4. But Be Careful With Z-Scores
Financial data often isn't nicely Gaussian.
Volatility can have:

fat tails
regime shifts
clustering
extreme observations

So blindly calculating a global mean and standard deviation can be problematic.
A better approach can be a rolling normalization.
For example:
[Z_t=\frac{X_t-\mu_{t,N}}{\sigma_{t,N}}]
where the statistics are calculated using the previous (N) observations.
That allows the feature to adapt to changing market conditions.

5. Momentum Feature
Let's construct a normalized MACD feature.
Raw MACD depends heavily on the instrument's price scale.
Instead consider:
[MACD%=\frac{MACD}{P}\times100]
Then calculate its rolling standardized value:
[Z_{MACD,t}
\frac{MACD%t-\mu{MACD%,t}}{\sigma_{MACD%,t}}]
Now MACD becomes comparable across different price levels.

6. Histogram Dynamics
The histogram itself:
[H_t=MACD_t-Signal_t]
is useful.
But its change is also useful:
[\Delta H_t=H_t-H_{t-1}]
And potentially:
[\Delta^2H_t
\Delta H_t-\Delta H_{t-1}]
So we can construct:
Histogram level
      ↓
Histogram velocity
      ↓
Histogram acceleration

But remember our earlier precision:

(\Delta H) is a change in histogram, not literally a physical second derivative of price.

It's a feature describing momentum dynamics.

7. RSI Features
Instead of one RSI number:
[RSI_t]
create:
[RSI_t]
[\Delta RSI_t]
[\Delta^2 RSI_t]
and perhaps distance from neutral:
[RSI_{neutral}=RSI_t-50]
Now you can distinguish:
Case A
[RSI=65,\quad\Delta RSI=+5]
Strong and strengthening.
Case B
[RSI=65,\quad\Delta RSI=-5]
Still strong, but weakening.
Case C
[RSI=48,\quad\Delta RSI=+8]
Currently neutral-ish, but rapidly improving.
These are three different market states.

8. CCI
For CCI:
[CCI_t]
and:
[\Delta CCI_t]
give us displacement and displacement change.
You can additionally use a bounded transformation if you want to prevent extreme CCI values from dominating a model.
For example:
[CCI_{bounded}=\tanh\left(\frac{CCI}{k}\right)]
where (k) controls how quickly the feature saturates.
The hyperbolic tangent satisfies:
[-1<\tanh(x)<1]
So even CCI = +700 doesn't completely overwhelm everything else.

9. ADX as Organization
We can similarly represent ADX relative to a baseline.
For example:
[ADX_f=\frac{ADX-25}{25}]
and separately:
[\Delta ADX]
This gives us two pieces of information:

How organized is the market?

and:

Is organization increasing or decreasing?

That's much better than a single ADX > 25.

10. ATR Should Be Relative
Use:
[ATR%=\frac{ATR}{P}]
Then compare current volatility to recent volatility.
For example:
[VolRatio=\frac{ATR_t}{Median(ATR_{t-N:t-1})}]
Interpretation:
[VolRatio<1]
means current volatility is below its recent baseline.
[VolRatio>1]
means current volatility is above its recent baseline.
For example:
[VolRatio=0.6]
suggests compression.
While:
[VolRatio=1.8]
indicates substantial expansion.

11. DeMarker
DeMarker already has a bounded range:
[0\le DeM\le1]
So normalization isn't as urgent.
A simple centered feature:
[DeM_f=2DeM-1]
maps:
[0\rightarrow-1]
[0.5\rightarrow0]
[1\rightarrow+1]
Then:
[\Delta DeM]
captures the change.

12. Now Build the State Vector
Our market at time (t) might become:
[X_t=[S_t,RSI_f,\Delta RSI,MACD_f,\Delta MACD_f,H_f,\Delta H,CCI_f,\Delta CCI,ADX_f,\Delta ADX,VolRatio,DeM_f,\Delta DeM]]
where (S_t) represents price structure.
Now we have something mathematically meaningful:
[X_t\in\mathbb{R}^{14}]
The market is represented as a point in a 14-dimensional feature space.

13. What Does That Mean?
Imagine only three features:
[X=[RSI_f,ADX_f,VolRatio]]
Every candle becomes a point:
              ADX
               ↑
               │       ●
               │   ●
               │ ●
               │
               └────────────→ RSI
              /
             /
         Volatility

Different market states occupy different regions.
For example:
LOW ADX + LOW ATR
        ↓
     RANGE

RISING ADX + RISING ATR
        ↓
   TREND BIRTH

HIGH ADX + HIGH RSI
        ↓
 TREND / MATURITY

HIGH ATR + LOW ADX
        ↓
VOLATILITY SHOCK

Now we're thinking in terms of state space rather than individual indicators.

14. Continuous Bullish Score
We can create:
[S_t=w^TX_t]
where:
[w=[w_1,w_2,\dots,w_n]]
is the vector of feature weights.
Expanded:
[S_t=w_1RSI_f+w_2\Delta RSI+w_3MACD_f+w_4\Delta MACD+w_5H_f+w_6\Delta H+\cdots]
A positive score could represent bullish conditions.
A negative score could represent bearish conditions.
But there's an important problem.

15. Correlated Features
MACD and histogram aren't independent.
RSI and CCI aren't independent.
ATR and volatility-derived features aren't independent.
ADX and directional movement are related.
So if you blindly assign:
[w_i=1]
you may effectively count the same information multiple times.
For example:
MACD
  ↓
Signal
  ↓
Histogram

These aren't three independent observations.
Likewise:
RSI
CCI

both contain information about directional price movement.
This is why feature analysis matters.

16. Information vs Redundancy
Suppose we discover:
MACD → predictive
Histogram → predictive
RSI → predictive
CCI → almost no incremental information
DeMarker → useful during breakouts
ADX → very useful for regime classification

Then CCI shouldn't necessarily be discarded completely.
It may still be useful in a particular regime.
This leads to a much better question:

Does feature (X) provide information about future returns after conditioning on the other features?

Formally, we're interested in something resembling:
[I(X;Y\mid Z)]
where (I) represents conditional mutual information.
You don't need to calculate this yet.
Just understand the concept:

Does this feature tell us something new?


17. Score Is Not Probability
Suppose:
[S=0.8]
Do NOT say:

"80% chance price goes up."

That would be nonsense unless the score had specifically been calibrated as a probability.
A score is simply a model output.
To obtain:
[P(R_{t+h}>0)]
we need a probabilistic model.

18. Logistic Model
A simple starting point is logistic regression.
Define:
[p_t=P(Y_t=1\mid X_t)]
where:
[Y_t=\begin{cases}1 & R_{t,h}>0\0 & R_{t,h}\le0\end{cases}]
Then:
[p_t=\frac{1}{1+e^{-z_t}}]
where:
[z_t=w^TX_t+b]
This transforms any real-valued (z) into:
[0<p<1]
So the model could output:
[p=0.63]
meaning an estimated 63% probability under the model and its calibration assumptions.
Not prophecy.
A conditional statistical estimate.

19. Turning Probability Into a Trade
Now we can define:
[p=P(R_{t,h}>0\mid X_t)]
But probability alone still isn't enough.
Suppose:
[p=0.60]
If winners average:
[+0.5R]
and losers average:
[-1R]
then:
[E=(0.60)(0.5)-(0.40)(1)]
[E=0.30-0.40]
[E=-0.10R]
So a 60% win probability can still produce a losing strategy.
Expected payoff matters.

20. This Gives Us the Full Quantitative Chain
We now have:
[\boxed{Market\rightarrowFeatures\rightarrowState\rightarrowProbability\rightarrowExpected Return\rightarrowRisk\rightarrowTrade}]
More formally:
[X_t\rightarrowP(Y_t=1|X_t)\rightarrowE[R|X_t]\rightarrowPositionSize]
That is the core architecture behind a large class of systematic approaches.

21. A Critical Research Rule
Never normalize using future information.
Suppose you're calculating the mean RSI:
[\mu]
for candle (t).
You cannot use:
RSI from candles t+1
RSI from candles t+2
...

because those values weren't available at (t).
Your transformation must obey:
[X_t=f(Data_{\le t})]
not:
[X_t=f(Data_{t-100:t+100})]
The first is causal.
The second leaks the future.

22. Our Strategy Is Evolving
Version 0.1
Hard rules:
ADX > 25
MACD > Signal
ΔMACD > 0
RSI > 50
CCI > 0
DeM > 0.5
...

Version 0.2
Continuous features:
RSI deviation
RSI velocity
MACD deviation
MACD velocity
Histogram velocity
CCI deviation
ADX deviation
ADX velocity
ATR regime
DeMarker deviation
...

Version 0.3
Probabilistic model:
[P(R_{t+h}>0|X_t)]
Version 0.4
Expected-value model:
[E[R_{t+h}|X_t]]
Version 0.5
Decision system:
[\text{Trade only if}\quadE[R|X]>TransactionCost+RiskPremium]
Now we're getting somewhere.

23. One More Crucial Concept: Stationarity
A feature relationship is useful only if it remains sufficiently stable.
Suppose:
[P(R>0|RSI=65)=0.62]
during one period.
But later:
[P(R>0|RSI=65)=0.49]
The relationship changed.
Markets are non-stationary.
That means:

The statistical properties of the process can change over time.

This is one reason regime modeling matters.
The relationship:
[P(Y|X)]
may actually be:
[P(Y|X,Regime)]
So RSI = 65 in a range and RSI = 65 in a strong trend can represent very different conditional probabilities.

24. The Architecture We Are Building
                 MARKET
                    │
                    ▼
             RAW OHLCV DATA
                    │
                    ▼
              FEATURES
                    │
          ┌─────────┴─────────┐
          ▼                   ▼
      PRICE STATE          INDICATORS
          │                   │
          └─────────┬─────────┘
                    ▼
             REGIME MODEL
                    │
                    ▼
             FEATURE VECTOR
                    │
                    ▼
          PROBABILITY MODEL
                    │
                    ▼
           EXPECTED VALUE
                    │
                    ▼
             RISK ENGINE
                    │
                    ▼
               EXECUTION
                    │
                    ▼
             PERFORMANCE
                    │
                    ▼
              RESEARCH LOOP

And that is the point where technical analysis becomes quantitative research rather than a collection of colored lines.

Next: Lesson 25
Feature Engineering + Regime Detection in Practice
We'll build the actual mathematical definitions for:

Range detection
Compression detection
Trend birth
Trend expansion
Trend maturity
Exhaustion
Volatility shock
Feature interactions such as:[RSI\times ADX][MACD\times ATR][\Delta H\times\Delta ADX]
A proper regime classifier
How to avoid creating hundreds of useless correlated features

Then we'll have enough structure to move into an actual backtesting implementation rather than merely philosophizing about one.

Lesson 25: Feature Engineering + Regime Detection
Now we build the part that makes the previous lessons actually useful: detecting what kind of market we're in before interpreting the indicators.
The central idea:
[\boxed{\text{Indicator meaning is conditional on market regime}}]
RSI 70 in a range is not the same state as RSI 70 during a strong trend. Same number, different probability distribution. Markets enjoy this particular form of ambiguity.

1. What Is a Regime?
A market regime is a period in which the statistical/structural behavior of the market is relatively consistent.
For our framework, we'll use:
[\mathcal{R}_t\in{Range,\ Compression,\ TrendBirth,\ TrendExpansion,\ TrendMaturity,\ Exhaustion,\ VolatilityShock}]
The regime is not directly observable.
We infer it from observable features:
[X_t=f(OHLCV_{\le t})]
So:
[\boxed{\mathcal R_t=g(X_t)}]

2. Don't Start With Indicators
The first features should come from price itself.
Why?
Because indicators are transformations of price.
If you build your entire regime detector from seven derivatives of the same underlying series, you can accidentally convince yourself that seven independent pieces of evidence agree.
They don't.
Start with:

returns
range
volatility
trend slope
structural highs/lows
volume if available

Then add indicators.

3. Return
Define logarithmic return:
[r_t=\ln\left(\frac{P_t}{P_{t-1}}\right)]
For small returns:
[r_t\approx\frac{P_t-P_{t-1}}{P_{t-1}}]
Why use log returns?
Because they have convenient additive properties:
[\ln\frac{P_t}{P_{t-2}}
\ln\frac{P_t}{P_{t-1}}+\ln\frac{P_{t-1}}{P_{t-2}}]
So multi-period log return is the sum of individual log returns.

4. Realized Volatility
Given a rolling window of (N) returns:
[\sigma_t
\sqrt{\frac{1}{N-1}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}(r_{t-i}-\bar r_t)^2}]
This gives us empirical movement variability.
Now we have an independent volatility feature in addition to ATR.
Why keep both?
Because:

ATR uses True Range
realized volatility uses return dispersion

They measure related but not identical things.
That distinction can become useful.

5. Compression
A compression regime means movement has become unusually constrained.
One simple measure:
[CompressionRatio_t=\frac{ATR_t}{Median(ATR_{t-N:t-1})}]
If:
[CompressionRatio<1]
volatility is below its recent baseline.
If:
[CompressionRatio\ll1]
the market is particularly compressed.
But don't immediately define:
[Ratio<0.7\Rightarrow breakout]
That's a hypothesis, not a law of nature.
Compression can resolve into:

bullish expansion
bearish expansion
continued compression
violent two-sided movement


6. Trend Slope
Use a rolling regression:
[P_{t-i}=\alpha+\beta i+\epsilon_i]
The coefficient:
[\beta]
measures directional slope.
But raw (\beta) depends on price scale.
Normalize it:
[SlopeNorm=\frac{\beta}{P_t}]
Now positive values indicate upward slope and negative values downward slope.

7. Trend Strength Needs More Than Slope
Consider:
[SlopeNorm>0]
but:
[ADX=12]
Price is drifting upward, but directional organization is weak.
Compare:
[SlopeNorm>0]
and:
[ADX=38]
Now the same positive slope exists inside a much more organized directional environment.
Therefore:
[TrendState=f(SlopeNorm,ADX)]
rather than:
[TrendState=f(SlopeNorm)]

8. Range Detection
A simple range environment might have:
[|SlopeNorm|\approx0]
[ADX<20]
and relatively low volatility:
[ATRRatio\approx1]
or below its recent baseline.
Additionally, price should repeatedly interact with similar upper and lower boundaries.
So conceptually:
[Range_t=I(|SlopeNorm|<s)\cdotI(ADX<a)\cdotI(Volatility< v)]
where (I(\cdot)) is an indicator function:
[I(condition)=\begin{cases}1,&condition\ true\0,&condition\ false\end{cases}]
This is useful for an initial rule-based classifier.

9. But Hard Thresholds Have a Problem
Suppose:
[ADX=19.9]
and:
[ADX=20.1]
A binary classifier says:
19.9 → RANGE
20.1 → NOT RANGE

That's absurdly discontinuous.
The underlying market didn't undergo a spiritual transformation between 19.9 and 20.1.
This motivates soft classification.

10. Soft Features
Instead of:
[ADX>25]
we can define a smooth function.
For example, the logistic function:
[\sigma(x)=\frac{1}{1+e^{-x}}]
Then:
[TrendProbability
\sigma(k(ADX-25))]
where (k) controls steepness.
Now:
ADX low       → probability near 0
ADX ≈ 25      → probability around 0.5
ADX high      → probability near 1

This is much more flexible.

11. Trend Birth
Trend birth should involve change, not simply high ADX.
A candidate trend-birth state:
[ADX_t \text{ low/moderate}]
but:
[\Delta ADX_t>0]
while:
[|\text{SlopeNorm}| \uparrow]
and:
[ATRRatio_t>1]
and momentum begins moving directionally.
For bullish trend birth:
[SlopeNorm>0]
[\Delta ADX>0]
[\Delta MACD>0]
[\Delta H>0]
[ATRRatio>1]
This is:

movement becoming directional and organized.


12. Trend Expansion
Trend expansion is stronger.
We want:
[ADX \uparrow]
[|SlopeNorm|\uparrow]
[ATRRatio>1]
plus directional momentum.
For bullish expansion:
[MACD>0]
[\Delta MACD>0]
[RSI>50]
[\Delta RSI>0]
[CCI>0]
[\Delta CCI>0]
The key word is:
coherence
The features are moving in the same general direction.

13. Trend Maturity
Eventually the trend becomes established.
Typical characteristics:
[ADX \text{ high}]
[Price\ structure\ intact]
but perhaps:
[\Delta ADX\le0]
[\Delta H<0]
[\Delta RSI<0]
[\Delta CCI<0]
[\Delta DeM<0]
This doesn't mean reversal.
It means:

The trend remains strong, but internal acceleration is deteriorating.

That's maturity.

14. Exhaustion
Exhaustion becomes more convincing when multiple dimensions deteriorate simultaneously.
For bullish exhaustion:
[Price\ still\ makes\ HH]
while:
[\Delta RSI<0]
[\Delta MACD<0]
[\Delta H<0]
[\Delta CCI<0]
[\Delta DeM<0]
and:
[\Delta ADX<0]
Now the trend is producing new highs with increasingly weak internal characteristics.
That's a warning state.
Not a short signal.

15. Volatility Shock
This is a particularly important regime.
Suppose:
[ATRRatio\gg1]
and:
[RealizedVolRatio\gg1]
but:
[ADX]
remains low or unstable.
Then:
[High\ Volatility + Low\ Organization]
is more consistent with a volatility shock than a clean trend.
Possible behavior:
Large up candle
↓
Large down candle
↓
Large up candle
↓
Large down candle

ATR says:

enormous movement.

ADX says:

little persistent directional organization.

Those are not contradictory.
They describe different dimensions.

16. Feature Interaction
Here's where feature engineering becomes more interesting.
Individual features tell us one thing.
Interactions tell us what happens when two conditions coexist.
Consider:
[RSI\times ADX]
Suppose:
[RSI=70]
If:
[ADX=12]
the environment may be range-like.
But:
[ADX=45]
suggests strong directional organization.
Therefore the same RSI can have different significance depending on ADX.

17. Another Interaction
Consider:
[\Delta H\times\Delta ADX]
Case 1
[\Delta H>0]
[\Delta ADX>0]
Momentum acceleration and trend organization are both increasing.
Potentially strong expansion.
Case 2
[\Delta H>0]
[\Delta ADX<0]
Momentum is accelerating, but overall trend organization is weakening.
That could represent:

short-lived impulse
countertrend move
transition
late-stage burst

Same histogram behavior.
Different regime.

18. MACD × ATR
Consider:
[MACD>0]
with:
[ATRRatio<1]
Price has bullish momentum while volatility is compressed.
Potential interpretation:

controlled directional movement.

Compare:
[MACD>0]
with:
[ATRRatio\gg1]
Now momentum exists during unusually large movement.
Potential interpretation:

aggressive expansion.

Again, the combination contains more information than either feature alone.

19. Interaction Explosion
There is a trap.
If you have:
[20]
features, pairwise interactions produce:
[\frac{20(19)}{2}=190]
features.
Three-way interactions produce hundreds more.
You can very quickly create:
14 indicators
↓
500 features
↓
3 years of data
↓
beautiful backtest
↓
complete statistical garbage

This is feature explosion.
More features do not automatically mean more information.

20. Feature Selection
A feature should survive several tests.
Test 1: Economic/market rationale
Why should this feature contain information?
Test 2: Stability
Does its relationship persist across time?
Test 3: Incremental value
Does it add information beyond existing features?
Test 4: Out-of-sample performance
Does it work on data not used during development?
Test 5: Robustness
Does small parameter variation destroy the result?
If:
[RSI>61.37]
works brilliantly but:
[RSI>61.3]
fails catastrophically, something is probably wrong.

21. Regime Classifier v0.1
Let's construct a basic rule-based classifier.
RANGE
Approximately:
[ADX<20]
[|SlopeNorm|\approx0]
and no significant volatility expansion.

COMPRESSION
[ATRRatio<0.8]
and:
[ADX\text{ low}]
and:
[|SlopeNorm|\text{ low}]

TREND BIRTH
[\Delta ADX>0]
[ATRRatio>1]
[|SlopeNorm|\uparrow]
with directional momentum beginning to align.

TREND EXPANSION
[ADX>25]
[\Delta ADX>0]
[|SlopeNorm|>threshold]
and directional momentum remains coherent.

TREND MATURITY
[ADX\ high]
but:
[\Delta ADX\le0]
and several momentum features begin deteriorating.

EXHAUSTION
Price continues making directional extremes while several internal features deteriorate.

VOLATILITY SHOCK
[ATRRatio\gg1]
[RealizedVolRatio\gg1]
while directional organization is weak/unstable.

22. The Classifier Should Not Pretend to Know
There is an important distinction:
[\boxed{\text{Regime classification is inference, not observation}}]
We don't observe:

"TREND_EXPANSION"

directly.
We observe:
prices
returns
ranges
ADX
ATR
RSI
MACD
CCI
DeMarker

and infer:
[P(Regime=r|X_t)]
Eventually, instead of saying:
TREND_EXPANSION = TRUE

we could say:
[P(TREND_EXPANSION)=0.78]
[P(RANGE)=0.08]
[P(EXHAUSTION)=0.10]
[P(SHOCK)=0.04]
That is a much richer representation.

23. Why Regime Probability Matters
Suppose our trading model predicts:
[P(R_{t+h}>0)=0.63]
But:
[P(RANGE)=0.75]
Maybe the continuation strategy should not trade.
Another observation:
[P(R_{t+h}>0)=0.63]
with:
[P(TREND_EXPANSION)=0.80]
Now the same directional prediction exists in a much more suitable regime.
So the decision can become:
[P(Return>0\mid X,Regime)]
rather than merely:
[P(Return>0\mid X)]

24. Our Architecture Is Now Becoming Hierarchical
                    MARKET
                      │
                      ▼
                RAW FEATURES
                      │
              ┌───────┴───────┐
              ▼               ▼
          PRICE DATA       INDICATORS
              │               │
              └───────┬───────┘
                      ▼
               FEATURE ENGINE
                      │
                      ▼
               REGIME MODEL
                      │
        ┌─────────────┼─────────────┐
        ▼             ▼             ▼
      RANGE         TREND          SHOCK
                      │
                      ▼
                 SETUP MODEL
                      │
                      ▼
              RETURN PREDICTION
                      │
                      ▼
                RISK ENGINE

This is considerably closer to a serious research system.

25. The Quantitative Mindset
At this stage, don't ask:

"Is this indicator good?"

Ask:

"Under which conditions does this feature contain predictive information?"

For example, perhaps research eventually discovers:
[RSI]
is weak by itself, but:
[RSI\times ADX]
is useful.
Or:
[DeMarker]
has little predictive value during established trends but becomes useful around breakout failures.
Or:
[\Delta H]
is useful primarily when:
[ADX>25]
Those are much more sophisticated conclusions.

26. The State Machine We Can Now Measure
We started with:
RANGE
  ↓
TREND BIRTH
  ↓
TREND EXPANSION
  ↓
TREND MATURITY
  ↓
EXHAUSTION
  ↓
TRANSITION

Now each arrow can become a measurable transition.
For example:
[P(TrendExpansion_{t+1}\mid Compression_t)]
or:
[P(Exhaustion_{t+1}\mid TrendMaturity_t)]
Now we're not merely identifying states.
We're studying state transitions.
That is the next major conceptual jump.

27. Lesson 25 Takeaway
Your indicators now have a hierarchy:
Raw market
[Price,\ Return,\ Range,\ Volume]
↓
Derived measurements
[RSI,\ MACD,\ CCI,\ ADX,\ ATR,\ DeMarker]
↓
Dynamic features
[\Delta X,\ \Delta^2X]
↓
Relative features
[Z(X),\ ATRRatio,\ VolRatio]
↓
Interaction features
[RSI\times ADX]
[\Delta H\times\Delta ADX]
↓
Market state
[P(Regime|X)]
↓
Prediction
[P(R_{t+h}>0|X,Regime)]
↓
Decision
[Expected\ Value]
↓
Risk
[PositionSize,\ Stop,\ Exposure]
That's the complete conceptual pipeline we've been building since MACD Part 1.

Lesson 26: State Transitions + Backtesting the Regimes
Next we move from "what state are we in?" to:
[\boxed{\text{"What happens after this state?"}}]
We'll construct transition matrices such as:
[P(R_{t+1}=j\mid R_t=i)]
and test questions like:

How often does compression become a trend?
How often does trend birth fail?
How long do expansion regimes last?
What happens to returns after exhaustion?
Which indicator changes occur before state transitions?
Which transitions have positive expectancy?
How do we backtest all of this without look-ahead bias?

That is where the indicator framework starts turning into a genuine market-state research model.

Lesson 26: State Transitions + Backtesting Regimes
We now move from:

"What regime are we in?"

to:

"What does this regime tend to become next?"

This is a major shift.
A static classifier says:
[R_t = \text{TREND_EXPANSION}]
A transition model asks:
[P(R_{t+1}=j\mid R_t=i)]
That gives us a probabilistic model of market-state evolution.

1. The State Variable
Let:
[S_t\in{R,C,B,E,M,X,V}]
where:

(R) = Range
(C) = Compression
(B) = Trend Birth
(E) = Trend Expansion
(M) = Trend Maturity
(X) = Exhaustion
(V) = Volatility Shock

The exact labels aren't sacred. They're our research taxonomy.
Now every candle receives a state:
t0    RANGE
t1    RANGE
t2    COMPRESSION
t3    COMPRESSION
t4    TREND_BIRTH
t5    TREND_EXPANSION
t6    TREND_EXPANSION
t7    TREND_MATURITY
...


2. Transition Matrix
Suppose we count what happens after every state.
For example, imagine historical data produced:



Current
Range
Compression
Birth
Expansion
Maturity
Exhaustion
Shock



Range
0.60
0.25
0.08
0.03
0.00
0.00
0.04


Compression
0.20
0.35
0.25
0.12
0.00
0.00
0.08


Birth
0.05
0.05
0.20
0.55
0.10
0.00
0.05


Expansion
0.02
0.01
0.05
